Inc., 1992 1 \n    33→IN I - TECHNOLOGY ~ \n    34→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    35→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    36→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    37→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    38→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    39→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    40→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    41→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    42→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    43→below: \n    44→SURFACES MUST BE \n    45→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    46→II MUST BE \n    47→11 SMALL \n    48→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    49→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    50→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    51→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    52→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    53→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    54→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    55→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    56→MATERIALS \n    57→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    58→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    59→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n    60→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n    61→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n    62→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n    63→solderability. \n    64→MACHINES \n    65→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n    66→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n    67→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n    68→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n    69→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n    70→able. \n    71→MANPOWER \n    72→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n    73→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n    74→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n    75→operating it. \n    76→METHODS \n    77→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n    78→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n    79→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n    80→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n    81→including all data that pertains to it. \n    82→ENVIRONMENT \n    83→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n    84→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n    85→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n    86→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n    87→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n    88→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n    89→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n    90→again may cause a defect. \n    91→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n    92→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n    93→Producing high quality solder joints \n    94→requires that five general process \n    95→variables need to be in control; the \n    96→materials, machines, manpower, \n    97→methods, and the environment. \n    98→2 Section I - Technology \n    99→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   100→I I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I \n   101→In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   102→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   103→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   104→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   105→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   106→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   107→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   108→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   109→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   110→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   111→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   112→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   113→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   114→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   115→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   116→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   117→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   118→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   119→there is considerable truth to what was said. \n   120→I \n   121→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   122→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   123→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   124→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   125→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   126→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   127→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   128→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   129→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   130→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   131→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   132→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   133→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   134→must first be defined and then maintained. \n   135→280- \n   136→260- \n   137→240- \n   138→T 220- \n   139→High quality, low defect soldering \n   140→requires identifying the optimum \n   141→temperature profile for reflowing \n   142→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   143→over and over. \n   144→I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n   145→30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \n   146→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   147→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   148→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   149→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   150→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   151→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   152→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   153→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   154→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   155→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   156→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   157→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   158→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   159→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   160→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   161→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   162→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   163→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   164→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   165→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   166→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   167→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   168→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   169→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   170→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   171→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   172→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   173→Section I - Technology 3 \n   174→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   175→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   176→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   177→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   178→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   179→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   180→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   181→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   182→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   183→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   184→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   185→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   186→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   187→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   188→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   189→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   190→three zones. \n   191→reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   192→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   193→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   194→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   195→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   196→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   197→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   198→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   199→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   200→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   201→set at the same value. \n   202→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   203→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   204→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   205→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   206→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   207→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   208→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   209→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   210→200 c \n   211→I PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW \n   212→I \n   213→I \n   214→I \n   215→I \n   216→I \n   217→I \n   218→I \n   219→I \n   220→I \n   221→I \n   222→I \n   223→COOLING I \n   224→TIME \n   225→4 Section I - Technology \n   226→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   227→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   228→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   229→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   230→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   231→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   232→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   233→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   234→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   235→SOLDER SPATTER \n   236→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   237→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   238→FICIENT DRYING. \n   239→BURNT BOARDS, \n   240→DEWET SOLDER \n   241→fi COLD SOLDER \n   242→JOINTS, DULL \n   243→SOLDER JOINTS \n   244→(TOO COLD) \n   245→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   246→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   247→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   248→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   249→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   250→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   251→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   252→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   253→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   254→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   255→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   256→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   257→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   258→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   259→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   260→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The \n   261→joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   262→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   263→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   264→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   265→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   266→DEFINE THE BAND \n   267→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   268→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   269→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   270→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   271→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   272→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   273→results. \n   274→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   275→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   276→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   277→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   278→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   279→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   280→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   281→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   282→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   283→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   284→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   285→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   286→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   287→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   288→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   289→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   290→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   291→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   292→must be exposed. \n   293→The total temperature deviation from \n   294→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   295→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   296→the band. \n   297→Section I - Technology 5 \n   298→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   299→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   300→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   301→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   302→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   303→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   304→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   305→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   306→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   307→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   308→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   309→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   310→AT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \n   311→WHERE: \n   312→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   313→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   314→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   315→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   316→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   317→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   318→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   319→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   320→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   321→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   322→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   323→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   324→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   325→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   326→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   327→profile. \n   328→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   329→J \n   330→mmmm \n   331→mmmm \n   332→mmmm \n   333→mmmm \n   334→mmmm \n   335→mmmm \n   336→OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   337→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   338→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   339→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   340→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   341→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   342→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   343→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   344→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   345→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   346→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   347→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   348→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   349→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   350→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   351→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   352→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   353→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   354→the figure. \n   355→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN \n   356→b \n   357→L \n   358→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   359→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   360→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   361→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   362→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   363→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   364→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   365→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   366→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   367→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   368→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   369→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   370→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   371→and product loading. \n   372→6 Section I - Technology \n   373→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   374→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   375→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   376→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   377→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   378→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   379→is through example. \n   380→CONVECTION \n   381→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   382→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   383→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   384→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   385→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   386→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   387→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   388→is actually transferring heat. \n   389→2ONVECTION I \n   390→- HOT . \n   391→AIR \\ \n   392→FLOW - COLD OBJECT IS \n   393→- HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   394→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   395→CONDUCTION \n   396→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   397→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   398→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   399→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   400→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   401→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   402→tial exists within the mass. \n   403→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   404→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   405→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   406→always present in signijicant quantities. \n   407→CONDUCTION \n   408→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   409→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   410→RADIATION \n   411→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   412→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   413→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   414→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   415→RADIATION I \n   416→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   417→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   418→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   419→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   420→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   421→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   422→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   423→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   424→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   425→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   426→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   427→Section I - Technology 7 \n   428→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   429→95.38 \n   430→Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   431→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   432→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   433→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   434→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   435→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   436→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   437→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   438→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   439→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   440→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   441→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   442→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   443→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   444→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   445→wavelength ranges. \n   446→111111lI \n   447→INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   448→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   449→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   450→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   451→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   452→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   453→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   454→follows: \n   455→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   456→WHERE: \n   457→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   458→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   459→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   460→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   461→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   462→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   463→Tt = Target temperature (OK) \n   464→(5.67 x w/cm2/”K4) \n   465→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   466→paste and circuit board material makes \n   467→IR an efficient means of heating. \n   468→The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   469→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   470→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   471→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   472→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   473→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   474→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   475→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   476→f IRSOURCE \n   477→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   478→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   479→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   480→FACTOR \n   481→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   482→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   483→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   484→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   485→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   486→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   487→absorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \n   488→absorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \n   489→I \n   490→loooo t \n   491→! 97.39 I4 I \n   492→96’70 96.04 I! \n   493→Control of infrared is generally done by controlling the \n   494→source temperature. Providing that the overall source \n   495→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   496→repeatability. \n   497→8 Section I - Technology \n   498→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   499→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   500→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   501→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   502→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   503→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   504→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   505→is important and what is not important. \n   506→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   507→The following is the general equation for convection \n   508→heating or cooling: \n   509→Q=HxAT \n   510→WHERE: \n   511→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   512→H = Convective film coefficient \n   513→AT = Temperature difference between \n   514→(W/cm2-”C) \n   515→fluid and object (“C) \n   516→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   517→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   518→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   519→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   520→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   521→proportionally. \n   522→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   523→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   524→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   525→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   526→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   527→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   528→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   529→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   530→~ ~~ ~~ \n   531→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   532→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   533→which enhances heat transfer. \n   534→very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   535→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   536→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   537→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   538→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   539→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   540→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   541→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   542→is shown below: \n   543→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   544→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 \n   545→- \n   546→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   547→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   548→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   549→HEAT TRANSFER \n   550→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   551→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   552→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   553→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   554→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   555→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   556→perpendicular flow convection: \n   557→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   558→FLOW DIRECTION \n   559→ttt \n   560→NO BOUNDARY \n   561→LAYER \n   562→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   563→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   564→TRANSFER \n   565→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   566→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   567→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   568→cooling results. \n   569→Section I - Technology 9 \n   570→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   571→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   572→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   573→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   574→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   575→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   576→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   577→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   578→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   579→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   580→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   581→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   582→CONVECTION \n   583→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   584→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   585→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   586→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   587→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   588→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   589→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   590→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   591→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   592→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   593→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   594→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   595→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   596→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   597→CONVECTION \n   598→Less sensitive to shading \n   599→Heats more uniformly \n   600→AT (Source-target) is small \n   601→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   602→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   603→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   604→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   605→chance of solder wicking. \n   606→is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   607→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   608→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   609→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   610→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   611→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   612→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   613→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   614→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   615→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   616→IR \n   617→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   618→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   619→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   620→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   621→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   622→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   623→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   624→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   625→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   626→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   627→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   628→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   629→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   630→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   631→system. \n   632→IR \n   633→Control source temperature only \n   634→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   635→Easy to control rate of rise \n   636→More sensitive to shading \n   637→Less uniform high mass differences \n   638→AT (Source-target) is large \n   639→10 Section I - Technology \n   640→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   641→~ \n   642→Ease of cleaning flux \n   643→residues (RMA) \n   644→Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   645→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   646→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   647→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   648→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   649→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   650→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   651→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   652→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   653→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   654→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   655→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   656→500 ppm \n   657→FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   658→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   659→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   660→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   661→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   662→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   663→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   664→RATIO = 6 / D \n   665→WHERE: \n   666→D = Particle Diameter \n   667→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   668→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   669→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   670→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   671→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   672→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   673→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   674→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   675→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   676→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   677→This can only be done through proper oven design. \n   678→NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   679→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   680→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   681→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   682→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   683→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   684→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   685→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   686→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   687→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   688→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   689→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   690→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   691→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   692→bility of creating solder balls. \n   693→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   694→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   695→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   696→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   697→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   698→listed below: \n   699→DESCRIPTION I PURITY LEVEL I \n   700→Significant reduction \n   701→in board discoloration \n   702→10,000 ppm \n   703→Reduction in solder 200 ppm \n   704→balling (RMA) \n   705→Reflow of low-residue \n   706→solder paste \n   707→< 100 ppm \n   708→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   709→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   710→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   711→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   712→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   713→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   714→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   715→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   716→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   717→your process. \n   718→Section I - Technology 11 \n   719→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   720→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   721→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   722→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   723→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   724→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   725→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   726→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   727→ing paragraphs. \n   728→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   729→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   730→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   731→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   732→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   733→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   734→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   735→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   736→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   737→detailed description. \n   738→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   739→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   740→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   741→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   742→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   743→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   744→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   745→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   746→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   747→detailed description. \n   748→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   749→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   750→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   751→below: \n   752→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   753→(MINIMUM) - LOAD FACTOR \n   754→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   755→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   756→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   757→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   758→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   759→not lead you to the optimum decision. \n   760→In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   761→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   762→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   763→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   764→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   765→equation: \n   766→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH PROCESS - \n   767→SPEED - PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   768→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   769→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   770→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   771→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   772→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   773→at the gas inlet. \n   774→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   775→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   776→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   777→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   778→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   779→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   780→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   781→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   782→product. \n   783→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   784→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   785→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   786→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   787→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   788→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   789→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   790→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   791→asked during the selection process. \n   792→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   793→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   794→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   795→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   796→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   797→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   798→needed. \n   799→12 Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   800→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   801→DESCRIPTION \n   802→The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   803→all be considered when comparing equipment. When \n   804→multiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \n   805→proper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \n   806→This is especially important when comparing ovens that \n   807→utilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \n   808→operating costs can be significantly different. This \n   809→section describes various costs that may be included in \n   810→the analysis. \n   811→HOT GAS IR \n   812→EQUIPMENT COST \n   813→Often, the initial cost of the equipment is the only cost \n   814→considered when comparing equipment. It is certainly \n   815→important, although it is only a part of the total cost \n   816→picture. To make a fair comparison, all costs should be \n   817→analyzed with a common basis, such as annual cost. As \n   818→an example let’s look at amachine that costs $50,000 and \n   819→has an expected life of 5 years, with an interest rate of \n   820→10%. Assume the value of the equipment is zero at the \n   821→end of five years. The annualized cost of the equipment \n   822→is equal to the initial cost times the Capital Recover \n   823→Factor. (3 For an interest rate of 10% with a period of \n   824→5 years, the CRF value is equal to 0.2638. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n   908→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n   909→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n   910→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n   911→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n   912→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n   913→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n   914→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n   915→are defined specific to this system. \n   916→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n   917→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n   918→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n   919→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n   920→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n   921→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n   922→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n   923→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n   924→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n   925→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n   926→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n   927→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n   928→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n   929→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n   930→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n   931→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   932→II 4 Y \n   933→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   934→heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n   935→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n   936→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n   937→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n   938→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n   939→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n   940→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n   941→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n   942→constant conditions. \n   943→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n   944→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n   945→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n   946→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n   947→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n   948→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n   949→the dryout and reflow zone. \n   950→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n   951→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n   952→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n   953→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n   954→transfer. \n   955→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n   956→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n   957→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n   958→product, it is critical that both these variables \n   959→are controlled. \n   960→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 15 \n   961→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n   962→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n   963→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n   964→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n   965→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n   966→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n   967→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n   968→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n   969→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n   970→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n   971→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n   972→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n   973→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n   974→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n   975→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n   976→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n   977→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n   978→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n   979→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n   980→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n   981→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- \n   982→VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n   983→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n   984→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n   985→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n   986→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n   987→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n   988→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n   989→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n   990→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n   991→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n   992→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n   993→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n   994→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n   995→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n   996→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n   997→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n   998→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n   999→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n  1000→per hour). \n  1001→GAS FLOW \n  1002→INPUT FROM \n  1003→N2 OR AIR \n  1004→SOURCE \n  1005→GAS FLOW \n  1006→INPUT FROM \n  1007→N2 OR AIR \n  1008→SOURCE \n  1009→16 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1010→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n  1011→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n  1012→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n  1013→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n  1014→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n  1015→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n  1016→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n  1017→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n  1018→LAMP DEFINITION \n  1019→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n  1020→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n  1021→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n  1022→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n  1023→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n  1024→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n  1025→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n  1026→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n  1027→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n  1028→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n  1029→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n  1030→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1031→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1032→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1033→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1034→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1035→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1036→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1037→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1038→instantly after its removal. \n  1039→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1040→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1041→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1042→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1043→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1044→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1045→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1046→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1047→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1048→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1049→removal. \n  1050→RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1051→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1052→PRODUCT \n  1053→”‘7 \\ REFLF JON. $ RE-EMITTED IR \n  1054→VECTION 1 FLOW \n  1055→-- \\P \n  1056→PRODUCT \n  1057→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1058→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1059→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1060→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1061→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1062→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1063→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1064→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1065→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1066→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1067→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1068→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1069→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1070→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1071→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1072→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1073→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1074→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1075→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1076→repeatability off 2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1077→is within this defined band. \n  1078→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1079→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1080→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1081→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1082→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1083→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1084→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1085→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1086→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1087→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1088→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1089→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1090→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1091→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1092→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 17 \n  1093→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1094→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) \n  1095→Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1096→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1097→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1098→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1099→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1100→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1101→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1102→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1103→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1104→convection oven. \n  1105→REFLOW \n  1106→TOP (IR5) DRY TOP (IR3) PREFLOW TOP (IR4) \n  1107→NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1108→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1109→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1110→environment at low flow levels. The system uses a \n  1111→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1112→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1113→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1114→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1115→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1116→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1117→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1118→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1119→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1120→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1121→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) \n  1122→The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1123→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1124→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1125→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1126→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1127→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1128→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1129→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1130→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1131→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1132→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1133→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1134→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \n  1135→measured with the machine fully loaded with product. \n  1136→REFLOW \n  1137→BOTTOM (1R6) DRY BOTTOM (IR3) PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) \n  1138→~~~ ~ \n  1139→The amount of flow in the oven is more \n  1140→related to the quantity required to accom- \n  1141→plish a high purity nitrogen environment, \n  1142→which is considerably less than in the case \n  1143→of a convection oven. \n  1144→NOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \n  1145→ALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n  1146→18 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1147→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1148→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1149→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1150→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1151→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1152→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1153→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1154→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1155→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1156→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1157→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1158→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1159→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1160→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1161→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each \n  1162→rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1163→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1164→structural integrity. \n  1165→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1166→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1167→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1168→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1169→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1170→tend to warp. The patented system from \n  1171→Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1172→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. \n  1173→- TENSION STRAIGHTENS RAIL \n  1174→SPRING MAINTAINS TENSION \n  1175→REFERENCES \n  1176→1. “The Transformation of American Industry”, Productivity-Quality Systems, Inc.
